3. Court of Appeals 
GR: Decisions of the DOLE Secretary, 
NLRC, and BLR, when acting in their 
appellate jurisdiction, are not appealable to 
the Court of Appeals. The proper remedy is 
a Petition for Certiorari under Rule 65, upon 
a showing of grave abuse of discretion. 
 
It is incumbent upon the petitioner to 
establish that the tribunal acted in a 
capricious 
and 
whimsical 
manner 
amounting to lack or excess of jurisdiction. 
Grave abuse of discretion exists when 
discretion 
is 
exercised 
arbitrarily, 
despotically, or in a manner equivalent to 
lack of jurisdiction. (Philippine National Bank 
v. Gregorio) 
 
XPN: Rule 43 of the Rules of Court 
 
Decisions, orders, or awards of a Voluntary 
Arbitrator or Voluntary Arbitration Panel may 
be appealed to the Court of Appeals via a 
Petition for Review under Rule 43, which 
allows review of questions of fact, law, or 
mixed questions of fact and law. 
 
Appeal via Rule 65, Rules of Court 
Petition for certiorari  
 
A person may file a verified petition in the 
proper court, alleging the facts with certainty 
and praying that judgment be rendered 
annulling or modifying the proceedings of 
any tribunal, board or officer exercising 
judicial or quasi-judicial functions, and 
granting such incidental reliefs as law and 
justice may require:  
 
1. When such tribunal, board or officer 
has acted:  
 
a. Without or in excess of its or his 
jurisdiction, or  
b. With grave abuse of discretion 
amounting to lack or excess of 
jurisdiction; AND  
 
2. When there is no appeal, or any plain, 
speedy, and adequate remedy in the 
ordinary course of law. 
 
 
The petition shall be accompanied by:  
 
1. A certified true copy of the judgment, 
order or resolution subject thereof;  
2. Copies of all pleadings and documents 
relevant and pertinent thereto; and  
3. A sworn certification of non-forum 
shopping as provided in the third paragraph 
of Sec. 3, Rule 46. 
 
 
Petition for PROHIBITION 
 
A person may file a verified petition in the 
proper court, alleging the facts with certainty 
and praying that judgment be rendered 
commanding the any tribunal, corporation, 
board, officer or person, whether exercising 
judicial, quasi-judicial or ministerial functions 
to desist from further proceedings in the 
action or matter specified therein, or 
otherwise granting such incidental reliefs as 
law and justice may require: 
 
1. When the proceedings of such tribunal, 
corporation, board, officer or person are: a. 
Without or in excess of its or his jurisdiction; 
or b. With grave abuse of discretion 
amounting to lack or excess of jurisdiction, 
AND  
 
2. When there is no appeal or any other 
plain, speedy, and adequate remedy in the 
ordinary course of law. The petition shall be 
accompanied by:  
 
a. A certified true copy of the 
judgment, order or resolution subject 
thereof;  
b. Copies of all pleadings and 
documents relevant and pertinent 
thereto; and  
c. A sworn certification of non-forum 
shopping as provided in the third 


<!-- PAGE 185 -->

 
 
 
 
177 
paragraph of Sec. 3, Rule 46. 
 
Petition for mandamus  
 
A person may file a verified petition in the 
proper court, alleging the facts with 
certainty and praying that judgment be 
rendered commanding any tribunal, 
corporation, board, officer or person, 
immediately or at some other time to be 
specified by the court, to do the act 
required to be done to protect the rights 
of the petitioner, and to pay the damages 
sustained by the petitioner by reason of 
the wrongful acts of the respondent:  
 
1. When any tribunal, corporation, board, 
officer or person:  
 
a. Unlawfully neglects the performance 
of an act which the law specifically 
enjoins as a duty resulting from an 
office, trust, or station; or  
b. Unlawfully excludes another from the 
use and enjoyment of a right or office to 
which such other is entitled; AND  
 
2. When there is no other plain, speedy 
and adequate remedy in the ordinary 
course of law. 
 
4. Supreme Court 
All petitions should be initially filed in the 
Court of Appeals, in strict observance of the 
doctrine on the hierarchy of courts, as the 
appropriate forum for the relief desired. [St. 
Martin Funeral Home v. NLRC, G.R. No. 
130866 (1998)] 
Rule 45, Rules of Court 
Filing of petition with Supreme Court A party 
desiring to appeal by certiorari from a 
judgment or final order or resolution of the 
Court of Appeals, the Sandiganbayan, the 
Regional 
Trial 
Court 
or 
other 
courts 
whenever authorized by law, may file with the 
Supreme Court a verified petition for review 
on certiorari. The petition shall raise only 
questions of law which must be distinctly set 
forth. [Sec. 1] 
Since the Court of Appeals had jurisdiction 
over the petition under Rule 65, any alleged 
errors committed by it in the exercise of its 
jurisdiction would be errors of judgment 
which are reviewable by timely appeal, and 
not by a special civil action of certiorari.  
If the aggrieved party fails to do so within the 
reglementary period, and the decision 
accordingly becomes final and executory, he 
cannot avail himself of the writ of certiorari, 
his predicament being the effect of his 
deliberate inaction. [Tirazona v Phil EDS 
Techno-Service Inc, G.R. No. 169712 
(2009)] 
Note: Review of decisions of the NLRC shall 
be done through (in order):  
1. Motion for reconsideration  
2. Rule 65 to the CA  
3. Rule 45 to the SC 
B. PROCEDURE AND REMEDIES 
1. Procedural Standards in Labor 
Proceedings – LC, Art. 227 
 
a. Burden of Proof; b. Quantum of 
Evidence 
In termination cases, the onus of proving the 
validity of dismissal lies with the employer.  
The quantum of proof which the employer 
must discharge is substantial evidence or 
that amount of relevant evidence as a 
reasonable mind might accept as adequate 
to support a conclusion, even if other minds, 
equally reasonable, might conceivably opine 
otherwise.  


<!-- PAGE 186 -->

 
 
 
 
178 
In the absence of a clear, valid, and legal 
cause for the termination of employment, the 
law considers the dismissal illegal and the 
burden is on the employer to prove that the 
termination was for a valid or authorized 
cause under the Labor Code.  
Also, it is not incumbent upon dismissed 
employees to prove their innocence of the 
employer's accusations against them. In 
other words, they have mandatory duty to 
forward evidence to prove that they did not 
commit any misfeasance or malfeasance in 
the office. [SPC Power Corp. v. Santos 
(2020)] 
 
c. Technical Rules not Binding 
In any proceeding before the Commission or 
any of the Labor Arbiters, the rules of 
evidence prevailing in courts of law or equity 
shall not be controlling and it is the spirit and 
intention of this Code that the Commission 
and its members and the Labor Arbiters shall 
use every and all reasonable means to 
ascertain the facts in each case speedily and 
objectively, without regard to technicalities of 
law or procedure, all in the interest of due 
process.  
In any proceeding before the Commission or 
any Labor Arbiter, the parties may be 
represented by legal counsel but it shall be 
the duty of the Chairman, any Presiding 
Commissioner or Commissioner or any 
Labor Arbiter to exercise complete control of 
the proceedings at all stages. 
Any provision of law to the contrary 
notwithstanding, the Labor Arbiter shall exert 
all efforts towards the amicable settlement of 
a labor dispute within his jurisdiction on or 
before the first hearing. The same rule shall 
apply to the Commission in the exercise of its 
original jurisdiction. 
 
2. Single-Entry Approach – D.O. No. 
151, s. 2016 
Single Entry Approach (SEnA) refers to the 
administrative approach to provide an 
accessible, 
speedy, 
impartial, 
and 
inexpensive settlement procedure of all labor 
and employment issues through a 30-day 
mandatory conciliation-mediation. 
SEnA was institutionalized by virtue of the 
Republic Act No. 10396 by the then 
President Benigno S. Aquino III last 14 
March 2013. By 2016, an Implementing 
Rules 
and 
Regulations 
through 
the 
Department Order No. 151 series of 2016 
was issued by the Department of Labor and 
Employment (DOLE) requiring the National 
Conciliation and Mediation Board (NCMB) 
and its Regional Branches, as an attached 
agency of DOLE, to establish a Single-Entry 
Assistance Desks (SEADs). 
The role of the NCMB, as one of the 
implementing agencies, is to provide a 
speedy, 
impartial, 
inexpensive, 
and 
accessible settlement of all labor issues 
arising from employer-employee relations, 
promote the use of conciliation-mediation in 
the settlement of all labor cases, and 
strengthen cooperation and coordination 
between 
and 
among 
DOLE 
agencies 
involved in dispute settlement. 
Who may file. Request for Assistance (RFA) 
may be filed by any aggrieved person such 
as employer, worker, including kasambahay, 
or group of workers, whether local or 
overseas, or a union, workers association or 
federation. In case of absence or incapacity 
of the aggrieved person, his/her immediate 
family with Special Power of Attorney (SPA) 
may file the RFA. In case of death, his/her 
legitimate heirs may file the RFA. 
In the setting of NCMB, Requesting Parties 
are classified into six (6) categories such as 
Individual Worker, Group of Workers, Union, 
Overseas 
Filipino 
Workers, 
Kasambahay/Family Driver, and Employer. 


<!-- PAGE 187 -->

 
 
 
 
179 
Where to file. SEnA RFA may be submitted 
via Onsite and Online Filing. For Onsite 
Filing, Requesting Parties may file their 
request at the NCMB Central Office or at any 
Regional 
Conciliation 
and 
Mediation 
Branches. For Online Filing, Requesting 
Parties may visit the Online Services Portal 
of the website to submit a complaint. After 
submission of complaint, Requesting Party 
will be notified and contacted by NCMB 
personnel for necessary actions. 
 a. Mandatory Conciliation-Mediation – 
R.A. No. 10396 
Republic Act No. 10396 amended the Labor 
Code by introducing a new Article 228, which 
institutionalizes 
mandatory 
conciliation-
mediation as the primary mode of dispute 
settlement for all labor and employment 
issues, 
subject 
to 
specific 
statutory 
exceptions. The law reinforces the State 
policy of encouraging voluntary, speedy, 
and non-litigious resolution of labor 
disputes 
before 
resort 
to 
formal 
adjudication. 
Under 
Article 
228(a), 
all 
labor 
and 
employment disputes must first undergo 
conciliation-mediation, and adjudicatory 
bodies such as the Labor Arbiters or 
appropriate offices of the Department of 
Labor and Employment may entertain only 
cases that have been duly endorsed or 
referred by an authorized officer, except in 
cases expressly excluded by law or by the 
Secretary of Labor and Employment. 
Article 228(b) recognizes party autonomy by 
allowing either or both parties to pre-
terminate 
the 
conciliation-mediation 
proceedings at any time and request referral 
to the proper DOLE office or tribunal with 
jurisdiction. It also allows the parties, by 
mutual agreement, to submit unresolved 
issues to voluntary arbitration, by reinforcing 
voluntary 
arbitration 
as 
a 
preferred 
alternative dispute resolution mechanism. 
RA 10396 does not abolish adjudication of 
labor 
cases; 
rather, 
it 
strengthens 
conciliation-mediation as a mandatory 
procedural step, without impairing the 
right of parties to pursue their claims 
before the proper forum if settlement 
fails. The law also authorizes the Secretary 
of Labor and Employment to issue the 
necessary 
implementing 
rules 
and 
regulations to ensure effective enforcement. 
3. Procedure in the NLRC 
a. Appeal from the RAB (Regional 
Arbitration Branch) to the NLRC Proper 
Decisions, awards, or orders of the Labor 
Arbiter become final and executory unless 
appealed to the Commission within ten (10) 
calendar days from receipt by the party of 
the Labor Arbiter’s decision, award, or order.  
Grounds. The grounds for appeal from a 
Labor Arbiter to the NLRC are strictly limited 
and are expressly provided under Article 223 
of the Labor Code. 
An appeal may be entertained only on any of 
the following grounds: 
1. Prima facie evidence of abuse of 
discretion on the part of the Labor 
Arbiter. 
2. Decision, order, or award was 
secured through fraud or coercion, 
including graft and corruption. 
3. Pure questions of law. 
4. Serious errors in the findings of facts 
which, if not corrected, would cause 
grave or irreparable damage or injury 
to the appellant. 
i. Perfection 
Manner of perfecting an appeal. The 
manner of perfecting an appeal is governed 
by Section 3(a), Rule VI of the NLRC New 
Rules of Procedure. 


<!-- PAGE 188 -->

 
 
 
 
180 
To perfect an appeal, the appellant must, 
within the reglementary period, file an appeal 
under oath, with proof of payment of the 
required appeal fee, and submit a verified 
Memorandum of Appeal stating the grounds 
relied upon, the arguments in support 
thereof, the relief prayed for, the date of 
receipt of the assailed decision, and proof of 
service upon the adverse party. A mere 
notice of appeal, without compliance with 
these requisites, does not stop the running of 
the appeal period. 
In cases involving a monetary award, Article 
223 further requires that an appeal by the 
employer may be perfected only upon the 
posting of a cash or surety bond, issued by a 
reputable bonding company duly accredited 
by the Commission, in an amount equivalent 
to the monetary award. 
Simultaneous compliance. Accordingly, an 
appeal is perfected only by the simultaneous 
compliance with the following within the ten 
(10)-day reglementary period: filing of the 
notice 
of 
appeal, 
submission 
of 
the 
memorandum of appeal, and posting of the 
required appeal bond, when applicable.  
Note: Failure to comply with any of 
these 
requirements 
renders 
the 
Labor Arbiter’s decision final and 
executory. 
ii. Reinstatement or Execution Pending 
Appeal 
Concept of Reinstatement. It is the 
restoration of the Ee to the state from which 
he has been unjustly removed or separated 
without loss of seniority rights and other 
privileges. The person reinstated assumes 
the position he had occupied prior to his 
dismissal, and is, ordinarily, entitled only to 
the last salary in that position. (Azucena, 
2016) 
Reinstatement 
presupposes 
that 
the 
previous position from which one had been 
removed still exists, or that there is an 
unfilled position which is substantially 
equivalent or of similar nature as the one 
previously occupied by the Ee. 
Forms of Reinstatement 
1. Actual or physical. The Ee should 
be reinstated to his position which he 
occupies prior to his illegal dismissal 
under the same terms and conditions 
prevailing prior to his dismissal or 
separation or, if no longer available, 
to a substantially equivalent position. 
 
2. Payroll. The Ee is merely reinstated 
in the payroll. The Ee although not 
admitted 
back 
to 
work, 
would 
nevertheless be included in the 
payroll and entitled to receive salary 
and other benefits as if she were in 
fact working. (Azucena, 2016) 
Note: Er is given the option to 
reinstate either actually or in 
payroll. 
Employer Can Reinstate an Employee in 
Payroll When Actual Reinstatement is No 
Longer Possible 
1. The Er believes that there was a valid 
cause for dismissal; or 
2. Er does not want to see anymore an 
unwanted face in the company 
premises because it may demoralize 
Ees. 
Order of Reinstatement and Its Effects 
An order of reinstatement by the LA is not the 
same as actual reinstatement of a dismissed 
or separated Ee, however it is immediately 
executory even pending appeal. Thus, until 
the Er continuously fails to implement the 
reinstatement aspect of the decision of the 
LA, their obligation to the illegally dismissed 
Ee, insofar as accrued backwages and other 
benefits 
are 
concerned, 
continues 
to 


<!-- PAGE 189 -->

 
 
 
 
181 
accumulate. It is only when the illegally 
dismissed Ee receives the separation pay (in 
case of strained relations) that it could be 
claimed with certainty that the Er-Ee 
relationship has formally ceased thereby 
precluding the possibility of reinstatement. 
(Triad Security & Allied Services, Inc. et al v. 
Ortega, G.R. No. 160871, 06 Feb. 2006) 
An order for reinstatement entitles an Ee to 
receive his accrued backwages from the 
moment the reinstatement order was issued 
up to the date when the same was reversed 
by a higher court without fear of refunding 
what he had received. (Pfizer v. Velasco, 
G.R. No. 177467, 09 Mar. 2011) 
Relevant Jurisprudence 
When reinstatement is no longer feasible, 
separation pay in lieu is required and is 
distinct from separation pay for redundancy.  
 
Under Article 294, an illegally dismissed 
employee is entitled to full backwages from 
dismissal until reinstatement, or until finality 
if reinstatement is impossible. Reinstatement 
here is barred by strained relations and the 
abolition of the position.  
 
(★McConnell Dowell Phils. v. Archimedes 
B. Bernal,  G.R. No. 224685, November 10, 
2021, as penned by J.Gaerlan) 
b. Injunction 
NO temporary injunction or restraining order 
in any case involving or growing out of a 
labor dispute shall be issued by any court or 
other entity.  
On the other hand, the Office of the 
President, the Secretary of Labor, the 
Commission, the Labor Arbiter or Med-
Arbiter may enjoin any or all acts involving 
or arising from any case pending before any 
of said offices or officials which if not 
restrained forthwith may cause grave or 
irreparable damage to any of the parties 
to the case or seriously affect social or 
economic stability. 
Incidental motions will not be given due 
course. In all proceedings at all levels, 
motions for dismissal or any other incidental 
motions shall not be given due course, but 
shall remain as part of the records for 
whatever they may be worth when the case 
is decided on the merits. 
4. Certiorari against DOLE Secretary 
and NLRC Proper Rulings 
Nature of Remedy 
 
Certiorari under Rule 65 is the proper judicial 
remedy to assail final rulings of the DOLE 
Secretary and the NLRC, since these rulings 
are generally final and unappealable in the 
ordinary sense.  
 
Under Rule 65 of the Rules of Court, a 
petition for certiorari lies only when: 
 
1. The tribunal, board, or officer 
acted 
without 
jurisdiction, 
in 
excess of jurisdiction, or with 
grave 
abuse 
of 
discretion 
amounting to lack or excess of 
jurisdiction; and 
2. There is no appeal or any plain, 
speedy, and adequate remedy in 
the ordinary course of law. 
 


<!-- PAGE 190 -->

 
 
 
 
182 
Grave Abuse of Discretion. Grave abuse of 
discretion refers to a capricious, whimsical, 
or arbitrary exercise of judgment equivalent 
to lack or excess of jurisdiction. Mere errors 
of judgment or appreciation of evidence are 
not sufficient. 
 
 
Rulings/Decisions/Resolutions of DOLE 
and the NLRC 
 
The DOLE Secretary, or duly authorized 
DOLE officials, may validly rule on matters 
falling within their statutory authority. In the 
exercise of such authority, they may 
incidentally determine the existence of an 
employer-employee relationship, when such 
determination is necessary for the resolution 
of the issue before them (e.g., visitorial and 
enforcement powers, certification election 
issues, or labor standards enforcement). 
These rulings are not subject to appeal, as 
no appeal is provided by law.  
 
Judicial recourse is available only through a 
Petition for Certiorari under Rule 65, and only 
upon a clear showing that the DOLE acted 
without or in excess of jurisdiction, or with 
grave abuse of discretion. 
 
Similarly, decisions and resolutions of the 
NLRC are, as a rule, final and unappealable 
under the Labor Code. Parties aggrieved by 
NLRC rulings cannot avail of an ordinary 
appeal. The sole judicial remedy is a Rule 65 
Petition for Certiorari, which is limited strictly 
to questions of jurisdiction or grave abuse of 
discretion, and does not permit a review of 
the merits, factual findings, or appreciation of 
evidence. 
 
In both instances, certiorari is not a 
continuation of the appeal process, but an 
extraordinary remedy available only when 
the tribunal’s action is tainted by capricious, 
whimsical, or arbitrary exercise of judgment 
amounting to lack or excess of jurisdiction. 
Mere errors of judgment or conclusions of 
fact or law are insufficient to justify judicial 
intervention. 


<!-- PAGE 191 -->

 
 
 
 
183 
SUMMARY OF JURISDICTION 
 
I. ORIGINAL JURISDICTION 
Body / Office 
Nature of Jurisdiction 
Specific Cases Covered 
1. Labor Arbiter  
(NLRC - Regional 
Adjudication Board) 
Original and exclusive 
1. ULP cases.  
2. Termination disputes, including illegal dismissal and constructive dismissal.  
3. Cases involving wages, rates of pay, hours of work, and other terms and 
conditions of employment when accompanied by a claim for reinstatement.  
4. Claims for actual, moral, exemplary (AME), and other damages arising from ER–
EE relations.  
5. Cases arising from violations involving legality of strikes and lockouts.  
6. All other claims arising from ER–EE relations involving an amount exceeding ₱
5,000, whether or not accompanied by reinstatement.  
7. Non-compliance with compromise agreements entered into before the Labor 
Arbiter.  
8. Execution of awards of Voluntary Arbitrators.  
9. Wage distortion disputes where there is no CBA.  
10. Overseas employment money claims and employment-related disputes under 
R.A. No. 8042, as amended. 
2. DOLE Regional 
Director  
Original and exclusive (visitorial 
and enforcement powers) 
1. Labor standards enforcement cases under Art. 128.  
2. Occupational safety and health conditions, including compliance orders such as 
stoppage or suspension of operations. 


<!-- PAGE 192 -->

 
 
 
 
184 
3. DOLE Regional 
Director / DOLE 
Hearing Officer  
Original and exclusive 
(recovery/adjudicatory) 
Recovery of wages, simple money claims, and other benefits not exceeding ₱5,000 
per employee or house helper, and not accompanied by a claim for reinstatement, 
resolved through summary proceedings. 
4. Secretary of 
Labor and 
Employment (SOLE) 
Original (special statutory 
authority) 
Assumption of jurisdiction over, or certification to NLRC of, labor disputes causing or 
likely to cause a strike/lockout in industries indispensable to the national interest 
(Art. 278[g]). 
5. NLRC 
(Commission Proper) 
Original  
Petitions for injunction or temporary restraining order in labor disputes in instances 
provided by law; compulsory arbitration of certified national interest labor disputes 
upon certification/referral by the SOLE (Art. 278[g]). 
6. National 
Conciliation and 
Mediation Board 
(NCMB) 
No adjudicatory power; 
conciliation-mediation and 
program administration 
Performs preventive mediation/conciliation, assists settlement of labor disputes, 
encourages voluntary approaches, administers voluntary arbitration program, and 
provides conciliation-mediation services (including maritime labor disputes where 
applicable). It cannot decide cases, issue awards, or render judgments. 
7. Voluntary 
Arbitrator / Panel of 
VAs 
Original and exclusive (by 
agreement / CBA / law) 
Unresolved grievances involving interpretation/implementation of the CBA, 
interpretation/enforcement of company personnel policies, and non-gross CBA 
violations; other labor disputes (including ULP and bargaining deadlocks) if the 
parties agree. 
8. Bureau of Labor 
Relations (BLR) 
Original 
Inter-union and intra-union disputes and other labor relations disputes, except those 
arising from implementation/interpretation of CBAs (which go to grievance 
machinery and/or voluntary arbitration). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 193 -->

 
 
 
 
185 
II. APPELLATE JURISDICTION AND JUDICIAL REMEDIES 
Body / Office 
Nature of Jurisdiction 
Specific Cases Covered 
1. NLRC (Divisions) 
Exclusive appellate jurisdiction 
Appeals from decisions, awards, or orders of Labor Arbiters; also appellate 
jurisdiction over decisions of DOLE Regional Directors under Art. 129, as stated in 
the notes. 
2. Bureau of Labor 
Relations (BLR) 
Director 
Exclusive appellate jurisdiction 
Appeals from Med-Arbiter decisions in inter-union and intra-union disputes and other 
related labor relations disputes; appeals from DOLE Regional Director decisions in 
cases within the RD’s original jurisdiction, as stated in the notes. 
3. Court of Appeals 
Judicial review (primarily Rule 
65; Rule 43 in VA cases) 
Rule 65 Petition for Certiorari is the proper remedy to assail final rulings of the DOLE 
Secretary and the NLRC (grave abuse of discretion, no appeal). Rule 43 Petition for 
Review applies to decisions, orders, or awards of a Voluntary Arbitrator or VA panel. 
4. Supreme Court 
Review on certiorari (Rule 45) 
Rule 45 Petition for Review on Certiorari from judgments/final orders/resolutions of 
CA and other courts when authorized by law; notes also emphasize hierarchy of 
courts and the St. Martin Funeral Home doctrine on routing NLRC review through 
the CA via Rule 65 before reaching the SC.
